# Nightlog

Append-only record of the loop in [LOOP.md](LOOP.md). Read top to bottom: what needs a human
first, then the numbers, then the chronology.

## Review needed

**Decided by the owner on 2026-10-15, after the hardening night.** weida-zmq's `ipc://` bind takes a live endpoint over as libzmq does (`BoundUnixSocket::bind_replacing`; weida's `bind_unix` and weida-nng's `ipc://` keep refusing a live listener, as NNG does). Kept as built: NNG REP/RESPONDENT drop a reply to a full pipe, MQTT truncates past the user-property cap, the broker's `PER_MESSAGE_OVERHEAD` stays 1024, `max_pipes_per_peer` stays 256. Changed: a broker consumer whose delivery stalls or fails at the transport gets its **connection** closed (`LIMIT_EXCEEDED`, via the new `weida::Consumer::close`) so its redial re-subscribes; a per-message refusal does not close it. Commits are pushed after the review pass, with the tip gated. Measured since: a message out for delivery holds about 1.7 KB beside its body, against the 1024 B charged (about 136-240 B while it waits in the queue).

**The hardening night of 2026-10-15 (B-323 to B-334): security and allocations, every crate, and six things want your eye.** Eleven read-only audits (one per crate family) and a 4-minute run of all 35 fuzz targets fed eleven fix slices; main `7b171f5`+. **(1) Three deviations from the reference implementations, each documented in its parity row:** an `ipc://` bind in weida-zmq no longer steals a live endpoint (libzmq unlinks it; the runtime now refuses a live listener, 074ffc9); weida-nng's REP and RESPONDENT drop a reply to a full pipe instead of waiting (a decision, not a measured NNG behaviour; NNG interop 11/11 still green); weida-mqtt truncates a delivery past the user-property cap and says so in `DeliveryProperties::truncated` instead of closing. **(2) Two numbers that change behaviour:** the broker's `PER_MESSAGE_OVERHEAD` is 1024 (was 256), so a queue holds about a quarter as many empty messages; weida-nng's `max_pipes_per_peer` defaults to 256, which a proxy with more honest clients behind one address must raise. **(3) The broker's admission order is completion order** now that payload reads run off the queue loop, and a consumer whose delivery write fails or stalls loses its subscription. **(4) `GuaranteeSet::intersect` no longer adopts one side's `durability`/`replicas`** (PROTOCOL §6.5 bullet added): a requirement on an axis the peer did not declare fails negotiation. **(5) MQTT enhanced AUTH now requires `Authenticator::finish`**, with no default, so every implementation verifies the server's final data. **(6) Left for the next session, filed:** B-334 (Python bindings, in flight at the end of the night), B-335 (weida-py and zmq-py), B-336 (leftovers with their failures), B-337 (allocation work that needs a type redesign). Interop run tonight: libzmq 4.3.5 19/19, NNG 11/11, rumqttd and rmqtt 24/24; the AMQP and NATS server interop suites were not run.

**0037 is built, B-304 to B-314, and three things want your eye** (2026-10-04, main

`..._is_cut_at_once` and asserts `superseded == 0`, because under your counting rule a copy whose
FIN found a full queue is cut at `finish` and counts once, under the queue. **(2) A supersession
can count a copy twice**: if an upper layer's task sees the successor before layer 0's does, the
copy counts once in `layers_cut` and once in `superseded` — the "counts in both" case your rule
allows, but racy rather than by design. **(3) B-315 restored a behaviour B-308 had dropped**:
an empty segment arrives again, as an empty layer-0 stream. tuco86/weida#2 is closed; 8d35ed8,
9bcb0e0 and a0bcf4f were discarded at your choice.

## Numbers

**B-009 — DATA header cost per message.**
`cargo bench -p weida --bench patterns -- header --warm-up-time 1 --measurement-time 3`
(two runs, release, loopback, AMD Ryzen 7 5800X). A 64-byte push is a **135 B** DATA frame
minimally and **215 B** with a `uint` sequence plus a `sha256:<64 hex>` producer name
(**+80 B**, +59.3 %); throughput **179.4 Kmsg/s** against **162.9 Kmsg/s**, i.e. **−9 %**,
against a run-to-run spread of ±3 % (p = 0.38-0.41). The producer key is the expensive half:
74 B as a hex string against 35 B as a 32-byte CBOR `bstr`. Recorded in IMPLEMENTATION.md §4.

**B-010 — reorder buffer under cross-stream reordering.**
`cargo test -p weida --test streams reverse_order_completion -- --nocapture` (debug,
loopback, five identical runs). N transfers opened, finished from last to first. Queued
back to back the reversal never reaches the receiver: N = 16 gives **0** out of dispatch
position and a **0**-deep buffer, N = 256 gives **90 of 256** and a peak of **84** (33 % of
the transfers in flight, first divergence at position 29) purely from concurrent dispatch.
Awaiting each FIN's transport receipt does reverse it: N = 16 gives **16 of 16** and the peak
**15 = N − 1**. So an eager reassembler's bound is N − 1 and it is reachable; the cap must be
a configured number. Recorded in IMPLEMENTATION.md §4.

**B-011 — what a connection to a peer costs.**
`cargo bench -p weida --bench connections -- --warm-up-time 1 --measurement-time 3` (release,
loopback, pinned trust, mTLS off, three runs). A cold handshake is **1.04-1.10 ms**; dialling
a peer the pool already holds is **3.8 µs**, about **280x** cheaper, because it is not a
connection at all. 64 handshakes in series take 65.6-73.9 ms with no degradation. Memory:
**0-4 KiB** per idle runtime, **488-596 KiB** per live connection at 2 and **750-850 KiB** at
64, covering both ends in one process. So 0002's second connection per peer costs about a
millisecond and under a megabyte for the pair, against the unbounded head-of-line coupling it
removes. Recorded in IMPLEMENTATION.md §4.

**B-012 — connections per dialled path.** Same command, same machine. Today the pool ignores
the path, so **256 dials share one connection** and cost **1.47 ms** in total. With one
connection per path as 0002 will have it: **277.7 ms** for 256 paths, **1.08 ms** each, **995
KiB** of RSS per connection for both ends — linear, no degradation with the count. Against
`max_connections = 8` the ninth dial fails with `Error::LimitExceeded` after the server
completes the handshake and closes with `LIMIT_EXCEEDED`. Consequence for B-017: the bulk tier
wants **lazy** per-path connections (256 paths would otherwise cost 278 ms of handshakes where
they cost 1.5 ms today) while the control connection stays eager.

**Regression caught in passing:** `connect/cold_handshake` is **+12 %** (1.02-1.10 ms to
1.18-1.24 ms, p = 0.00) since B-016, because `Exec::resolve` spawns a task and awaits a join
handle on every `connect` even for a literal `127.0.0.1`. Filed as **B-025**, not fixed inside
a measure item. Both recorded in IMPLEMENTATION.md §4. **Fixed by B-025:** back to
**1.05-1.09 ms**, `change: −11.6 % (p = 0.00)`.

**B-021 — segment matching in the fan-out path.**
`cargo bench -p weida --bench patterns -- filters` (release, loopback). One connection holds N
filters the topic matches none of, so a publish is N matcher calls and nothing else; N = 1
against N = 64 subtracts the fixed cost out. **14.7 ns per filter** for a literal mismatch,
**14.3 ns** for a middle `*`, **15.1 ns** for a trailing `#` — the shape does not matter, so
the grammar is not the cost, registry iteration is. Sixty-four filters cost ~**1 µs** of a
publish against **64.5 µs** for one matched eight-subscriber fan-out: under 2 %. The byte
prefix it replaced is 0.46 ns per comparison as a pure function, which is the honest scale —
and what it could not do at any price is express a segment boundary. Recorded in
IMPLEMENTATION.md §4.

**B-040 — the dedup key's allocation per call.**
`cargo bench -p weida --bench patterns -- dedup_key --warm-up-time 1 --measurement-time 3`
(two runs, release, 4096 entries over eight scopes). The allocation is real — **~10 ns** per
call, 41.2 ns against 31.4 ns on a miss once the key is built outside the probe — and
**removing it does not pay**: the candidate two-level table whose outer lookup borrows `&str`
measures **42.0-42.8 ns** against the flat key's **41.1-41.3 ns**, because a second hash
lookup costs exactly what the allocation cost. So the key stays as it is. Scale: 10 ns is
0.13 % of a 1 KiB push, and deduplication is opt-in. Recorded in IMPLEMENTATION.md §4 and
beside the field in `dedup.rs`.

**B-011/B-012 amendment (0011).** The "control connection stays eager" conclusion above is
superseded: the control tier is parked, so the numbers now argue only for the per-path split,
which is built.

**B-043 — what the ZMTP bridge costs, against no bridge.**
`cargo bench -p weida-zmtp-bridge --bench interop -- --warm-up-time 1 --measurement-time 3`
(release, loopback, one process; `direct` is the pure-Rust `zeromq` crate talking to itself
over TCP, `bridged` is that crate through the adapter to a weida endpoint — one TCP hop, one
QUIC hop, two protocol terminations). REQ/REP round trip: **20.4-20.6 µs** direct against
**80.9-81.4 µs** bridged at 1 KiB (4.0×), **437-441 µs** against **3.34-3.41 ms** at 1 MiB
(7.7×). PUSH one-way at 1 KiB: **4.28-4.31 µs** against **5.16-5.28 µs**, so **+0.93 µs** per
message for a whole ZMTP termination, a DATA header and a QUIC stream. The 1 MiB one-way rows
(202-224 µs against 1.43-1.45 ms) measure zmq.rs buffering into its own queue on one side and
real forwarding on the other, so they are recorded but not compared.
**Linear in size, no cliff**, which is what decided both numbers §11 was holding open:
`max_message_bytes` is **1 MiB** because the cap bounds memory rather than latency (the old
8 MiB against a default 1024 connections was 8 GiB nobody had chosen), and the reply deadline
**stays 10 s**, now as ~3000× the slowest exchange the cap allows. The PUB-side queue moved
from 1024 *messages* to 8 MiB of *bytes* for the same reason — a depth multiplies by the cap.
Recorded in IMPLEMENTATION.md §4.

**B-059 — what the local transports cost against QUIC.**
`cargo bench -p weida --bench transports -- --warm-up-time 1 --measurement-time 3` (two runs,
release, both ends in this process, loopback for the QUIC rows, same machine as above).
Req/Rep round trip at 1 KiB: **58.3-58.8 µs** QUIC, **13.2-13.5 µs** inproc (4.4×),
**50.6-50.8 µs** `AF_UNIX` (1.2×); at 1 MiB **2.70-2.73 ms**, **144-145 µs** (19×),
**266-312 µs** (9-10×). Push one-way at 1 KiB: **7.97-8.08 µs**, **2.03-2.07 µs**,
**24.0-24.1 µs**; at 1 MiB **1.36 ms**, **57.7-79.5 µs**, **130-147 µs**. A live local
connection costs **0-1761 B** of RSS against the **995 KiB** of a QUIC one (B-012) — two to
three orders of magnitude, at the low end below what RSS resolves.
**The row that decides something is the 1 KiB Push: `AF_UNIX` is 3× *slower* than loopback
QUIC** (24.0 µs against 8.0), because QUIC opens a stream inside a connection it already has
while `AF_UNIX` opens a connection — a fixed ~22 µs per message that is invisible at 1 MiB
and is most of the message at 1 KiB. So 0010 §4.2's "the OS connection is the stream" holds
for bulk and for in-process work and fails at high message rates over `AF_UNIX`; that is the
price of not reimplementing a stream layer, and it now has a number. Recorded in
IMPLEMENTATION.md §4.

**B-289 — voice beside bulk.**
`cargo run --release -p weida --example voice_beside_bulk` (two runs, 20 Mbit/s client to
server through an in-process shaper with a 64 KiB drop-tail queue, same machine). Voice p95
with bulk on a separate path: **81.6 / 91.7 ms** under CUBIC, **6.7 / 47.7 ms** under BBR, at
4-5 % against 9 % voice loss. On one path with CUBIC: p95 49.5 ms and **no** voice loss. BBR
cleared the pre-set 30 % bar on separate paths, so 0034 §6 now names it for a bulk profile
sharing a bottleneck with media; the default stays `Cubic`. Recorded in IMPLEMENTATION.md §4.

**B-346 — one SWARM fetch from one seeder.**
`cargo test -p weida --features swarm --release --test swarm_large -- --ignored --nocapture`
(two runs, 256 MiB in 1 MiB pieces through `delay_proxy` at 13 ms each way, `per_peer` 16,
`max_inflight` 32): a normal download of the same bytes on the same path **45.8 / 44.8 MB/s**,
the session fetch **50.8 / 55.3 MB/s** (1.11x / 1.23x), peak RSS 82.3 / 78.9 MiB.
`cargo bench -p weida --features swarm --bench swarm -- --warm-up-time 1 --measurement-time 3`
(64 MiB, plain loopback): **683 MiB/s** (662-701). griasdi's per-ask fetch was 4-10 MB/s
against 53.7 MB/s HTTPS on a 27 ms path; a session now matches a normal download there. Recorded
in IMPLEMENTATION.md §4.18.

**B-391 — LAN and loopback throughput, wohnzimmer to tuco-pc.**
`target/release/weida-perf serve` on tuco-pc, `run_jobs perf-windows-amd64` (pipeline #88, 1 GbE)
and `weida-perf client --server 127.0.0.1`: LAN tcp up/down **88.4 / 118.7 MB/s**, push-1/8
**86.7 / 86.6**, pull-1/8 **116.8 / 116.8**, swarm-1/4 **116.8 / 116.8**, raw quinn 86.1 / 115.4;
loopback weida 726-771 MB/s against raw quinn 742-775 (swarm-4 2121). Video at 50 Mbit over
the LAN: every frame, p99 **10.51 ms** (alone), 18.64 ms beside a push on the same connection.
Recorded in IMPLEMENTATION.md §4.19.

2026-09-11T13:20Z | B-044 | done a456f72 | decision **0011: server-initiated traffic is answered where the registration arrived**. The question 0002 §7 left open was two questions in one coat, which is why 0002 and 0008 each thought they had closed it: *which connections are one peer* (0008: the proved fingerprint, nothing else) and *which connection a side writes its own traffic on*, which nobody had answered. 0011 §4.1: the connection the registration arrived on — and the sheets are unanimous, so this is the boring answer rather than an invention. EMQX sends a server-initiated PUBLISH "on the stream where it received that topic's subscription", a NATS `sid` is connection-local, a RabbitMQ consumer tag is channel-scoped, an AMQP 1.0 link is attached to one connection at a time and a second attach *steals* it. MQTT is the apparent counter-example and is not: its **session** owns the association, and a session is exactly what weida does not have. The general rule is the part worth keeping: **a frame that names a path rides that path's connection; a frame that names only the peer is peer-scoped.** **The consequence I had not seen when I filed the item:** that rule also takes the reserved L2 credit frame out of the control tier — it names a subscription, which names a path — amending 0003 §4.2, and leaves the tier with no cargo whatsoever. So the control connection is **parked**, not blocked, with a checkable revival condition (a genuinely peer-scoped latency-sensitive frame, or a measurement of the one residual coupling: an endpoint that publishes *and* subscribes on the same path). PROTOCOL §2.5 and §11, PATTERNS §1.3/§1.4, GUARANTEES, ARCHITECTURE §5, IMPLEMENTATION §4/§5, zmtp.md, SYNTHESIS §8.2 and decisions/README all updated; 0002 §6.3/§7 and 0003 §4.2 carry amendment markers rather than being rewritten. 2026-09-11T15:55Z | B-042 | done d6647db | the ZMTP **outbound bridge**, and the mirror is not symmetric — that is the finding. Inbound the bridge binds on the ZeroMQ side; outbound it binds the **weida** endpoint and dials the foreign peer, because the endpoint path is what weida applications address and an endpoint nobody can name is not reachable. Req/Rep dials **DEALER, not REQ**: a weida `Replier` takes concurrent exchanges and REQ is lockstep ("send and then receive exactly one message at a time"), so a REQ socket would serialize the concurrency this side offers. The 28/REQREP id frame is therefore what pairs a reply with its exchange, and the test drives four at once and has the peer answer them in reverse — two would have been a coin flip, since an id-blind bridge that answers whichever entry comes to hand guesses right half the time; with four it has to guess right four times, and it fails five runs in six. **One acceptance line could not be honoured as written and was re-derived rather than faked:** `ZMQ_ROUTER_MANDATORY` is an option on the ROUTER socket, and outbound that socket belongs to the peer — the adapter cannot set an option on a socket it does not own, so L5's silent drop arrives here as a reply that never comes. A per-exchange deadline turns it into `ERROR{NO_REPLY}`, which is a typed error rather than a hang, and that needed `IncomingRequest::refuse(code)` — the public API decision 0005 §4.3 always implied ("the ERROR frame is written by the application") and nothing had yet used, since until now an application could only refuse by dropping the handle. zmtp.md §8 L5 and §10 item 4 corrected, the deadline's value filed as a new §11 open question for the bench. The answer is a control connection per client — the first one it opens, carrying HELLO both ways and owning `Agreed`, the sequence scopes, the dedup window and the parked drain receipts — plus a **16-byte server-issued group token** on every further connection, admitted only when the token names a live peer **and** the kernel credentials match (uid always, pid where the platform reports one). The token names a group and proves nothing; the kernel says who is asking; neither alone admits a connection, which is the shape D-Bus's `nonce-tcp` and Tailscale's sandboxed Darwin fallback both use. `SCM_RIGHTS` lost on portability rather than taste: Windows `AF_UNIX` has no ancillary data, named pipes have no equivalent, and a protocol that needs it becomes what ipc.md §7 says Wayland is — unable to run on a transport that lacks it. So B-039 inherits the shape unchanged and is ready rather than blocked. 2026-09-14T09:40Z | review | fixed 50d5c6d..87cf7bb, filed B-245..B-251 | **A deep review of the whole tree — eight read-only audits, six parallel fixers — and the two sharpest findings were in code nobody had touched this week.** The question was code smells, guarantees against implementation, and how the result compares to ZeroMQ and nanomsg. Twenty-three defects were verified in the code before anything was changed, and four of them were mine from the three days before. **The worst: `Runtime::drain` closed nothing on three of four transports.** `DrainState::take` used `connections.drain(..)`, so `close_all` — which runs *after* it and is the only handle a local connection's close has [0010 §4.2] — locked an empty vector. On QUIC the endpoint close hid it; over inproc, `AF_UNIX` and named pipes a drained peer was never told, its `closed()` never resolved and its tasks held the connection for the life of the process. **Second: `PairOwner`'s claim was never released**, so a bound PAIR whose peer restarted — or merely went past the idle timeout — refused every reconnect with `LIMIT_EXCEEDED` forever, where both ZeroMQ and NNG accept the next peer; and a dialling pair never unregistered its path, so the second pair on a pooled connection got `AlreadyRegistered`. Both now have regression tests that fail on the old code. **Third: the client pool held its `async` mutex across DNS, every handshake attempt and the HELLO** — so one dial serialized every dial in the process, and `Runtime::close`, which takes the same lock, waited on a peer, which 0009 §4.4 forbids by name. The lock is now released for the dial and the race it admits is settled where it happens: whoever inserts first owns the key, the loser closes its own connection. **The pattern family cost four more.** A survey's deadline bounded only collection, not the asking, so one respondent that accepted a question and stopped reading held `survey()` open forever and every respondent behind it was never asked; a `SurveyRun` dropped at its deadline left one task and one bidirectional stream per respondent alive until the connection died; the bus queued a `Vec` **per member** with a message count and no byte budget (256 × body × members, unbounded in the payload), where Pub/Sub already shares one `Bytes` under a per-subscriber byte semaphore; and the bound half of a pair and every bus send omitted the producer sequence that `Peer::open` assigns, so one link could be numbered in one direction and unnumbered in the other under a negotiated `PerProducer`. The three one-way send paths are now one function, `stream::open_transfer_on`, which is what made the divergence visible in the first place. **The broker's own slice had two.** A CREDIT that overtook its own SUBSCRIBE was **dropped**, and nothing restates a grant, so that consumer starved silently — the queue's doc claimed the two frames cannot overtake each other, which is false, because they ride separate uni streams handled by separate tasks; and `report_accepted` awaited `open_uni` inside `serve_queue`, the queue's only driver, so one producer advertising zero uni streams could park admission and delivery for that path permanently. **A documented rule turned out to be unimplementable and was withdrawn rather than implemented**: "a consumer pauses by restating what it has already been delivered" cannot work against a monotone limit — `grant` ignores anything not strictly greater — and monotone is the half worth keeping, because it is what makes a reordered grant harmless. Four places said it; all four now say what the code does. **The foreign libraries cost four more, three of them one root cause**: a timer built inside a `loop` measures nothing, because every other `select!` arm's completion restarts it — AMQP's idle threshold was reset by *its own* traffic and its keep-alive tick never fired on a busy connection, and both bridges' expiry reapers needed 2.5 s of total silence to run, so `ERROR{NoReply}` never fired under load. weida-mqtt reset its keep-alive clock on an **inbound** QoS 0 PUBLISH, so a subscriber receiving traffic more often than once a minute never sent a PINGREQ and the server closed it at 1.5× Keep Alive: an ordinary subscriber, torn down every 90 s, looking exactly like a flaky broker. weida-nng's surveyor never woke a context whose answer a sibling had filed, and its per-pipe queues counted messages with no byte ceiling — the second dimension `weida-zmq` already had. **The fuzz set had stopped being a fuzz set**: `roundtrip` has not compiled since DATA key 8 landed (eight of twelve fields in a struct literal), and because the fuzz crate declares its own `[workspace]` no gate could see it. Repaired, plus the `credit` and `error` targets B-202 promised, and all nine run at 100 000 runs each — zero crashes, zero artifacts, recorded in IMPLEMENTATION §4. **`DataHeader::encode` could emit bytes its own decoder rejects** (an unsorted report array) and `wire_enum!` let a variant's wire value disagree with its declaration order, which is what `GuaranteeSet::intersect`'s `.min()` treats as the guarantee ladder — a mid-block insertion would have negotiated a guarantee weaker than the one reported. The first sorts, the second is now a `const` assertion that fails the build. **The comparison, honestly.** Against ZeroMQ and nanomsg the pattern semantics hold up — PAIR, SURVEY and BUS match their counterparts, and PAIR's refusal is *more* observable than ZeroMQ's silent drop — and the gaps are filed decisions rather than surprises (no reconnect, no fair queueing, no monitor, no proxy device). What the comparison actually surfaced is **ours and undocumented**: a 64-byte push is a 135-byte DATA frame, and the largest single item in it is a 55-byte `traceparent` that `data_header` writes unconditionally, minting a fresh root context when the caller supplied none — against ZMTP's one to nine bytes of framing and SP's eight. No decision note justifies it; it is filed as B-246 with the decision, not the deletion, as the deliverable. **One review claim did not survive verification and is worth recording as such**: the "answers lost to a coin flip at a spent deadline" race is unreachable at `remaining == 0`, because a `tokio` sleep of zero duration is not ready on its first poll — measured by forcing the deadline branch first with `biased;`, under which the old code still passed. The fix stands (the buffer is consulted first, and what the deadline branch finds it counts in `late` instead of dropping), but the test says what it pins rather than claiming a kill it does not make. Also fixed while in there: two destructors that unwrapped a poisoned mutex — a panic in a `Drop` during an unwind aborts the process, and neither a deque of receipts nor a map of senders has an invariant a panic could break — an 8 KiB array held across an await in every inbound stream's boxed future, `Consumer::open` paying for a stream before the header that can fail, and the local dispatcher interpreting SUBSCRIBE, CREDIT and CURSOR **before** negotiation, where a peer whose HELLO will be refused could still spend `max_subscriptions`. Seven items filed rather than fixed, each with its cost named: B-245 (`AF_UNIX` returns a truncated payload as a complete one — the transport's `finish` and `reset` are both `drop(writer)`, and Linux has no abort for a stream socket, which was *measured*, so the fix is framing), B-246, B-247 (per-subscriber fan-out unpriced), B-248 (one `unsafe` guard for seven crates that claim it), B-249, B-250, B-251. New `SimUnix` test fails before, passes after; PROTOCOL §2 states the close order; IMPLEMENTATION §6 keeps the full-binding case (second and later queued transfers need free places).
